Relative A inf A_{\Inf} -cohomology
Ildar Gaisin, Teruhisa Koshikawa

Abstract
We construct a relative version of the A inf A_{\Inf} -cohomology theory developed by Bhatt-Morrow-Scholze and relate it to the prismatic theory of Bhatt-Scholze. The construction relies on the fiber product of topoi. As an application we show that there is an étale comparison of the q q -crystalline pushforward after inverting μ ∈ A inf \mu\in A_{\Inf} .



1
Introduction

The goal of this paper is to construct and study an
A_{\Inf}
-cohomology theory associated to a
morphism
of
p
-adic formal schemes. The absolute case was initiated by Bhatt-Morrow-Scholze
[
BMS18
]
. However extending their theory to the relative setting is not a straightforward procedure and requires the consideration of an intermediary topos where the décalage functor operates. We recall the setup and (main) results of
[
BMS18
]
. Let
C
be a complete algebraically closed nonarchimedean extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
and residue field
k
. Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a proper smooth formal scheme over
\mathcal{O}
. As usual, let
\mathcal{O}^{\flat}
be the tilt of
\mathcal{O}
and
A_{\Inf}:=W(\mathcal{O}^{\flat})
be one of the period rings of Fontaine. Then the authors of
[
BMS18
]
construct an object

A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}}

which lives in
D^{\geq 0}(\mathfrak{X}_{\Zar},A_{\Inf})
. The main result (cf. Theorem 1.8 in loc.cit.) is that the
A_{\Inf}
-cohomology complex

R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}):=R\Gamma(\mathfrak{X}_{\Zar},A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}})

interpolates

(1)

the absolute crystalline cohomology of the mod
p
reduction of
\mathfrak{X}
and

(2)

the integral
p
-adic étale cohomology of the rigid-analytic fiber of
\mathfrak{X}
.

In particular,
R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X})
also interpolates the de Rham cohomology of
\mathfrak{X}
and the crystalline cohomology of the special fiber of
\mathfrak{X}
.

Originally the construction of
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
in
[
BMS18
]
lacked the framework of a site. This was addressed and solved in
[
BS22
]
with the definitions of the
q
-crystalline and prismatic sites. Since the introduction of the
q
-crystalline site it is more natural to directly compare
A_{\Inf}
-cohomology to
q
-crystalline cohomology (or to prismatic cohomology, depending on taste), and to refine (1) as (cf. Theorem 17.2 in loc.cit.)

(1)

R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X})
is isomorphic to the
q
-crystalline cohomology of
\mathfrak{X}
.

This is indeed a refinement as the absolute crystalline cohomology is the base change of the
q
-crystalline cohomology (cf. Theorem 1.8(5) and Theorem 16.14 in loc.cit.).
From this viewpoint, (2) may be regarded as a form of the étale comparison for the
q
-crystalline cohomology (or the prismatic cohomology).

Let us mention at this point, generalizing the work of
[
BMS18
]
, Česnavičius and one of the authors
[
CK19
]
were able to drop the smoothness assumption in
\mathfrak{X}
and work with a semistable model
1
1

1

In this situation the
C
is less general and the Zariski topology is not fine enough to treat the semistable case, so
\mathfrak{X}_{\Zar}
is replaced by
\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
.

. The generalization of (1)’ to the semistable setting was solved in
[
Kos21
]
(cf. Theorem 8.1 in loc.cit.).

In this paper we deal with the analogues of (1)’ and (2) in the
relative
setting under the smoothness assumption. The setup is as follows. Let
f\colon\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Y}
be a proper smooth morphism of flat
p
-adic formal schemes over
\mathcal{O}
. The flatness assumption is natural as
A_{\Inf}
-cohomology theory only remembers information coming from the generic fiber. There should be some assumptions on
\mathfrak{Y}
so that the adic generic fibers
X
and
Y
of
\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathfrak{Y}
respectively, can be defined, but let us ignore this point for now. We construct an object
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
(depending on
f
) and study the induced
relative

A_{\Inf}
-cohomology complex
R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y})
. Before we discuss the construction of these objects (including where they
should
live), let us make what information
R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y})
should capture more precise. For (2), as integral coefficients (e.g.
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
) are not a good coefficient theory for the étale topology, it is more convenient to work in the pro-étale setting. So the relative version of (2) becomes

(1)

R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y})

captures

Rf_{\eta,\proet*}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
,

where
f_{\eta}\colon X\to Y
is the induced morphism on the adic generic fibers, and
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}:=\varprojlim\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
as sheaves on the pro-étale site. For (1)’ and ignoring the notion of a
q
-PD pair for the moment,
[
BS22
]
construct a (big, global) site
q-\textnormal{CRYS}(\mathfrak{X}/A_{\Inf})
equipped with a structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}
(idem. for
\mathfrak{Y}
). The morphism
f
induces a cocontinuous functor of sites

f_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}\colon q-\textnormal{CRYS}(\mathfrak{X}/A_{\Inf})\to q-\textnormal{CRYS}(\mathfrak{Y}/A_{\Inf})

and so the relative version of (1)’ becomes

(1)

R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y})

captures

Rf_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}*}\mathcal{O}_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}
.

Let us now briefly review the construction of
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
(in the setting of
[
BMS18
]
), keeping in mind that it should be a complex whose cohomology interpolates between (1) and (2). On the site
X_{\proet}
, there is the sheaf
W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{X})
defined as the usual Fontaine period ring on affinoid perfectoid objects in
X_{\proet}
(cf.
[
Sch13
, Definition 6.1]
). For technical reasons one works with the derived
p
-adic completion of
W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{X})
, denoted by
\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
. There is a natural morphism of sites

\nu_{X}\colon X_{\proet}\to\mathfrak{X}_{\Zar}

and an initial approximation for
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
is the pushforward
R\nu_{X*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
. For an explanation of how to arrive at this approximation we refer the reader to
[
BMS18
, §1.4]
. As a consequence of the primitive version of a comparison theorem (cf.
[
Sch13
, Theorem 1.3]
), this complex
almost
captures the integral
p
-adic étale cohomology of
X
in a technical sense. One way to obtain a result on the nose is to invert a single element in
A_{\Inf}
whose image in
W(k)
is zero and this is essentially the content of
[
BMS18
, Theorem 1.8(iv)]
. Regarding the (
q
-)crystalline side, it will be important to modify
R\nu_{X*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
. To see why, let us review the “local” part of the argument of (1)/(1)’. The local computations are made in the case when
\mathfrak{X}
has a description by tori-coordinates and can be summarised by the following schematic:

\textstyle{R\nu_{X*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Faltings’
almost purity
(continuous) group cohomology
décalage
functor
q
-de Rham complex.

In this case, the upshot is that there is an explicit pro-(finite étale) affinoid perfectoid cover of the generic fiber (by repeatedly taking degree
p
Kummer extensions) and Faltings’ almost purity theorem means that
R\nu_{X*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
can be
almost
computed by the Koszul complex associated to the Galois group acting on the explicit cover. On the other hand an explicit (Koszul-like) complex calculating
q
-crystalline cohomology (in the context of (1)’) is the
q
-de Rham complex
2
2

2

In the context of (1), as one is interested in the (less refined) absolute crystalline cohomology the base change of the
q
-de Rham complex to
A_{\textnormal{crys}}
was considered
[
BMS18
, Corollary 12.5]
.
. In order to compare the Koszul complex almost representing
R\nu_{X*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
and the similarly-shaped
q
-de Rham complex one must kill the
non-integral
contribution
3
3

3

Also coined as the “junk torsion” in
[
BMS18
]
.
of the Koszul complex. To achieve this, we need a special element of
A_{\Inf}
. Fix a compatible system of primitive
p
-power roots of unity
\xi_{p^{r}}\in\mathcal{O}
. The system
(1,\xi_{p},\xi_{p^{2}},\ldots)
defines elements
\epsilon\in\mathcal{O}^{\flat}
,
\mu=[\epsilon]-1\in A_{\Inf}
, and let
L\eta_{\mu}
be the associated décalage functor. Then, in the case
\mathfrak{X}
is a torus, for instance, taking cohomology of the previous
almost
approximation and applying
L\eta_{\mu}
gives an honest quasi-isomorphism:

L\eta_{\mu}R\Gamma_{\cont}(\mathbb{Z}_{p},A_{\Inf}\{x^{\pm 1}\})\to L\eta_{\mu}R\Gamma(X_{\proet},\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}),

with the source identifying with the
q
-de Rham complex. This is one way of motivating the construction of

A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}}:=L\eta_{\mu}R\nu_{X*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}

and the passage from
R\nu_{X*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}\rightsquigarrow A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
is the main novelty of the approach of Bhatt-Morrow-Scholze. Let us also mention that several key inputs (albeit in a different setting) mentioned so far stretch back to works of Faltings
[
Fal88
,
Fal02
]
.

Let us now return to the relative situation. To get an idea of where
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
should live, say ?, the following two observations are key:

(1)

Since the (relative) cohomology of
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
should capture
Rf_{\eta,\proet*}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
, one idea is that there should be a map
?\to Y_{\proet}
.

(2)

By taking modulo
q-1
, the
q
-de Rham complex identifies with the usual de Rham complex. So we see that the (relative) cohomology of
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
should capture the usual de Rham complex
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
. As
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
lives in
D(\mathfrak{X}_{\Zar})
, there should be a map
?\to\mathfrak{X}_{\Zar}
.

So far (a)-(b) give us two maps
?\to Y_{\proet}
and
?\to\mathfrak{X}_{\Zar}
. Moreover as
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
should in principle only depend on
f:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Y}
, these two maps should be compatible with their projections to
\mathfrak{Y}_{\Zar}
. Finally it goes without saying that
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
should be some kind of relative cohomology of
\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
coming from
X_{\proet}
. So there should be a map
X_{\proet}\to?
. In summary there should be a commutative diagram (of topoi):

\textstyle{X_{\proet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f_{\eta,\proet}}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{f}}
\textstyle{?\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{X}_{\Zar}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{\proet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{Y}_{\Zar}.}

(1.1)

The shape of diagram (
1.1
) means that one candidate for ? is some kind of
product
of the topoi
Y_{\proet}
,
\mathfrak{X}_{\Zar}
and
\mathfrak{Y}_{\Zar}
. Indeed such a notion exists and is expounded in
[
ILO14
, Exposé XI]
. Moreover we choose to work with étale sites (although the same arguments work with the Zariski sites), so we take
?:=Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
. This leads to the following definition

A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}:=L\eta_{\mu}R\nu_{f*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}.

We caution the reader that in general, we can only prove that
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
is
almost
derived
p
-adically complete.

Having established where the décalage functor should operate, we define relative
A_{\Inf}
-cohomology as

R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}):=Rp_{*}A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}.

We can now state our first comparison result:

Theorem 1.1

(Comparison with the pro-étale pushforward)
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathfrak{Y}
be flat
p
-adic formal schemes locally of finite type over
\mathcal{O}
, and let
f\colon\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Y}
be a proper smooth morphism. Then

R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y})\otimes_{A_{\Inf}}^{\mathbb{L}}A_{\Inf}[\tfrac{1}{\mu}]\cong R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y})^{\wedge}\otimes_{A_{\Inf}}^{\mathbb{L}}A_{\Inf}[\tfrac{1}{\mu}]\cong Rf_{\eta,\proet*}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,Y}[\tfrac{1}{\mu}],

where
R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y})^{\wedge}
means the derived
p
-adic completion of
R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y})
.

The proof of Theorem
1.1
follows the same lines as its absolute analogue and the relevant relative comparison result was already proved by Scholze (cf. the morphism (
3.12
) which was proved to be an
almost
isomorphism in the the proof of
[
Sch13
, Theorem 8.8]
). The additional technical difficulty present in the relative case is proving that
Rf_{\eta,\proet*}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,Y}
is derived
p
-adically complete. In the absolute case (cf. the proof of
[
BMS18
, Theorem 5.7]
) the underlying objects are modules and integral étale cohomology is a perfect complex of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-modules. It turns out that
Rf_{\eta,\proet*}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
has enough finiteness properties so that the tensor product can still be controlled. It is based on the local constancy of
R^{i}f_{\eta,\et*}\mathbb{F}_{p}
proved in
[
SW20
, Theorem 10.5.1]
. This is also the source of the “locally of finite type” assumption on
\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathfrak{Y}
in Theorem
1.1
.

Without further mention, for the rest of the introduction we work in the setting of Theorem
1.1
. Although in the body of the paper we work with more general
\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathfrak{Y}
, the main obstacles are already present in the current setting.

We now push on with the comparison with
Rf_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}*}\mathcal{O}_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}
. First note that
R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y})
is a complex of
W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{Y})
-modules living in
Y_{\proet}
and this immediately raises the question of
where
this comparison should take place. Recall that there is the site
q-\textnormal{CRYS}(\mathfrak{Y}/A_{\Inf})
and the object
Rf_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}*}\mathcal{O}_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}
lives in the associated topos
(\mathfrak{Y}/A_{\Inf})_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}
. We construct a morphism of topoi

\mu_{\proet}:Y_{\proet}\to(\mathfrak{Y}/A_{\Inf})_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}},

(1.2)

which sends an affinoid perfectoid object to its pro-infinitesimal thickening given by Fontaine’s period ring (a
q
-PD pair). A similar morphism is used in the work of
[
MT20
]
when they relate relative Breuil-Kisin-Fargues modules with prismatic crystals (cf. proof of Theorem 5.15 in loc.cit.). It is along
\mu_{\proet}
that we make the comparison of
R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y})
and
Rf_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}*}\mathcal{O}_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}
. The next theorem represents the bulk of our work.

Theorem 1.2

(Comparison with the
q
-crystalline pushforward)
.

There is a canonical functorial morphism

\mu_{\proet}^{-1}Rf_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}*}\mathcal{O}_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}\to R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y})

(1.3)

in
D(Y_{\proet},W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{Y}))
compatible with the Frobenius such that the cohomology sheaves of the cone of (
1.3
)
are killed by
[\mathfrak{m}^{\flat}]\subset A_{\Inf}
(where
\mathfrak{m}^{\flat}\subset\mathcal{O}^{\flat}
is the maximal ideal).

We now discuss the proof of Theorem
1.2
. The starting point is constructing the
q
-crystalline analogue of the complex
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
. As a first attempt it is natural to consider the product of topoi
(\mathfrak{Y}/A_{\Inf})_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
and for the “
q
-analogue” of
\nu_{f}
(coming from the universal property of the product of topoi):

\nu_{f}^{q}\colon(\mathfrak{X}/A_{\Inf})_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}\to(\mathfrak{Y}/A_{\Inf})_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et},

one defines

q\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}:=R\nu_{f*}^{q}\mathcal{O}_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}.

The issue is that the underlying site of
(\mathfrak{Y}/A_{\Inf})_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
is difficult to describe due to the first projection
(\mathfrak{Y}/A_{\Inf})_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}\to\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}
essentially coming from a
cocontinuous
functor of sites. In particular it is difficult to describe the (local) sections of
q\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
. The fix is to work with the
oriented
product
(\mathfrak{Y}/A_{\Inf})_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}\overleftarrow{\times}_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
, which, at least heuristically, takes into account the asymmetric nature of the two projections to
\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}
. We make this precise, describing an explicit site for
(\mathfrak{Y}/A_{\Inf})_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}\overleftarrow{\times}_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
allowing us to study
q\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
(now defined with the oriented product in place of the usual product in the target of
\nu_{f}^{q}
).

With the correct setup in place, the next step in the proof of Theorem
1.2
is to compare both
q\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
and
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
. The morphism of topoi (
1.2
) extends to a morphism between the relevant products

\mu\colon Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}\to(\mathfrak{Y}/A_{\Inf})_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}\overleftarrow{\times}_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}

and the comparison between
q\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
and
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
takes the form:

Theorem 1.3
.

There is a canonical functorial isomorphism

\mu^{-1}q\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}\xrightarrow{\sim}A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}

(1.4)

in the relevant derived category compatible with the Frobenius.

The proof of Theorem
1.3
follows the same lines as its absolute analogue
[
BS22
, Theorem 17.2]
. One key ingredient is the comparison of
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
with the de Rham complex. However, in the relative situation, one has to work harder in order to compare the cohomological Koszul complex coming from continuous group cohomology with the (local) sections of
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
. In particular we show that affinoid perfectoid objects over
Y
are “rigid”, guaranteeing that the relevant continuous group cohomology groups have no non-zero
almost
-zero elements.

Returning to the proof of Theorem
1.2
, the morphism (
1.3
) is then obtained from pushing forward (
1.4
) to the pro-étale site
Y_{\proet}
, composed with a base change morphism. This base change morphism involves both products of topoi and is difficult to study on its own. However, after reducing coefficients from
A_{\Inf}
to
\mathcal{O}
, the situation becomes more manageable. In particular, one has Hodge-Tate comparisons for both
q\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
and
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
and it suffices to study two different base change morphisms, one for each of the product topoi. These two base change morphisms both involve the sheaf of Kähler differentials on
\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
. Both base change morphisms turn out to be
almost
isomorphisms and this, in short, ends the proof of Theorem
1.2
. Along the way we prove that the source of (
1.3
) is a perfect complex of
W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{Y})
-modules. As we are unable to do the same for
R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y})
, we cannot speculate whether (
1.3
) can be improved to an actual isomorphism.

We now discuss some applications of our comparison results: Theorems
1.1
-
1.2
. One immediate consequence is that relative
q
-crystalline/prismatic cohomology recovers the étale pushforward after inverting
\mu
:

Theorem 1.4
.

There is a quasi-isomorphism

(\mu_{\proet}^{-1}Rf_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}*}\mathcal{O}_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}})^{\wedge}\otimes_{A_{\Inf}}^{\mathbb{L}}A_{\Inf}[\tfrac{1}{\mu}]\cong Rf_{\eta,\proet*}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,Y}[\tfrac{1}{\mu}],

(1.5)

where
(\mu_{\proet}^{-1}Rf_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}*}\mathcal{O}_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}})^{\wedge}
denotes the derived
p
-adic completion of
\mu_{\proet}^{-1}Rf_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}*}\mathcal{O}_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}
.

To the best of our knowledge, this seems to be a version of an étale comparison which does not follow directly from
[
BS22
, Theorem 1.8(4) and Theorem 1.16(4)]
and is possibly one advantage of the theory constructed in this paper over prismatic cohomology. However the statement obtained by base changing (
1.5
) to a statement over
W(C^{\flat})
is closer to loc.cit. due to
[
BS21
, Proposition 3.6]
.

Another application is by imitating the strategy used in
[
AG22
]
, we are able to prove a
relative
Hodge-Tate spectral sequence:

Theorem 1.5
.

Suppose that for any
i,j\geq 0
,
R^{i}f_{\eta*}\Omega^{j}_{X/Y}
is locally free of finite type over
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
.
Then there is an
E_{2}
-spectral sequence

E_{2}^{i,j}=R^{i}f_{\eta*}\Omega^{j}_{X/Y}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(-j)\implies R^{i+j}f_{\eta,\proet*}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}

and it degenerates.

In their work, Abbes-Gros consider the “
topos de Faltings relatif
” (cf. §3.4 in loc.cit.) which plays a similar role to that of
Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
. The spectral sequences established in
[
CS17
, Corollary 2.2.4]
and
[
AG22
, Théorème 1.4.5]
, together with Theorem
1.5
give now a priori, three different Hodge-Tate filtrations on the rational
p
-adic étale higher direct images, albeit in different mutually non-exclusive settings. The generic analogue
Y_{\proet}\times_{Y_{\et}}X_{\et}
of our product of topoi can potentially be used to check the compatibility of the filtrations by Caraiani-Scholze and that of Theorem
1.5
. This would also involve checking the compatibility of the relative de Rham comparison in
[
Sch13
, Theorem 8.8]
and the one hypothetically implied by relative
A_{\Inf}
-cohomology theory, which we decided not to work out. One approach is to generalize the argument of
[
BMS18
, Theorem 13.1]
using
Y_{\proet}\times_{Y_{\et}}X_{\et}
.

Finally regarding torsion in the relative étale pushforward given that the de Rham pushforwards are locally free, we refer the reader to Theorem
7.4
. We give a proof which is based on the relative
A_{\Inf}
-cohomology theory (in particular Theorem
1.4
), however as noted by Takeshi Tsuji, the result essentially follows from the absolute case (cf. Remark
7.5
). We are not sure how to obtain a degree-wise result like in
[
BMS18
, Theorem 14.5(ii)]
.

Outline

We briefly explain the content of the different sections. In Section
2
we describe a basis for the fiber product
Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
. In Section
3
we establish some properties of lisse
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-sheaves on the pro-étale site in the context of complexes, and use this to prove Theorem
1.1
. In Section
4
we perform the local computations relating continuous group cohomology and
framed
sections of
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
. In Section
5
we use these local computations to relate
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
to the relative de Rham complex
\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}^{\bullet}
. In Section
6
we introduce the
q
-crystalline analogue
(\mathfrak{Y}/A_{\Inf})_{q-\textnormal{CRYS}}\overleftarrow{\times}_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
of
Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
, and together with the results in Section
5
, use this to prove Theorem
1.2
. Finally, in Section
7
, we prove some consequences including Theorems
1.4
-
1.5
.

Additional notation

Given
C
and
\mathcal{O}
as in the introduction, let
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathcal{O}
be its maximal ideal. We let
W(-)
denote the
p
-typical Witt vectors of a (not necessarily unital) ring so that
A_{\Inf}:=W(\mathcal{O}^{\flat})
is Fontaine’s ring. For instance we often consider the non-unital ring
W(\mathfrak{m}^{\flat})
. The ring
A_{\Inf}
comes equipped with the Frobenius
\varphi\colon A_{\Inf}\xrightarrow{\sim}A_{\Inf}
and de Rham specialization map
\theta\colon A_{\Inf}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}
. Its kernel
\ker(\theta)\subset A_{\Inf}
is generated by a single element
\xi:=\tfrac{[\epsilon]-1}{[\epsilon^{1/p}]-1}\in A_{\Inf}
and we denote
\tilde{\xi}:=\varphi(\xi)
. For
X
a locally noetherian adic space over
\Spa(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
we use the pro-étale site
X_{\proet}
constructed in
[
Sch13
]
. We adopt the notation for the various structure sheaves
4
4

4

Strictly speaking
\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
is a complex.
on
X_{\proet}
given in
[
BMS18
, Definition 5.4]
.

2
Preliminaries

The main goal of this section is to review the construction of the fiber product of topoi given in
[
ILO14
, Exposé XI]
(cf.
[
AGT16
, Chapter VI]
), relevant to our context. We also describe a suitable basis for the underlying site, which will facilitate extending the local computations performed in §
4
to sheaf-theoretic results such as the Hodge-Tate specialization in §
5
. The main result is Proposition
2.16
. We only urge the reader to understand the definition of relative
A_{\Inf}
-cohomology (cf. Definition
2.17
) and skip the remaining contents of this section upon first viewing.

2.1
Product of topoi:
Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}

In this section, unless otherwise stated,
\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathfrak{Y}
will be any
p
-adic formal schemes of type (S)(b) over
\mathcal{O}
, as in
[
Hub96
, §1.9]
. This means that for all
y\in\mathfrak{Y}
there exists an open affine neighborhood
\mathfrak{U}\subseteq\mathfrak{Y}
of
y
such that the topology on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(\mathfrak{U})
is
p
-adic and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(\mathfrak{U})[p^{-1}]
is strongly noetherian (idem. for
\mathfrak{X}
). Let
f\colon\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Y}
be any
\Spf(\mathcal{O})
-morphism. Note that in this situation the adic generic fibers
X
and
Y
of
\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathfrak{Y}
, respectively, are locally noetherian adic spaces. In particular the framework established in
[
Sch13
, §3]
applies. We begin by recalling the construction of the fiber product of topoi
Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
following the treatment in
[
ILO14
, Exposé XI]
. As explained in the introduction, this will be a crucial ingredient for our definition of relative
A_{\Inf}
-cohomology (cf. Definition
2.17
). We begin with a simple reality check on the objects involved.

Lemma 2.1
.

(1)

The sites
Y_{\proet}
,
\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}
and
\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
admit finite projective limits.

(2)

The natural functors
\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}\to Y_{\proet}
(obtained by taking the adic generic fiber) and
\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}\to\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
commute with finite projective limits and induce morphisms of sites

\nu_{Y}\colon Y_{\proet}\to\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}
and
f_{\et}\colon\mathfrak{X}_{\et}\to\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}
.

Proof.

(1)

Firstly
Y_{\proet}
admits finite projective limits due to
[
Sch13
, Lemma 3.10(vii)]
. By
[
Hub96
, §3.5]
, there is an equivalence of sites
\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}\cong\mathfrak{Y}_{\text{red},\et}
(idem. for
\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
), where
\mathfrak{Y}_{\text{red}}=(\mathfrak{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}/\mathscr{I})
is the reduced scheme underlying
\mathfrak{Y}
(here
\mathscr{I}
is the vanishing sheaf of ideals of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}
). Now
\mathfrak{Y}_{\text{red},\et}
has a final object (namely
\mathfrak{Y}_{\text{red}}
) and fiber products exist. Thus by
[
Sta18
, Tag 04AS, Lemma 4.18.4(1)
\iff
(3)]

\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}
(idem. for
\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
) admits finite projective limits.

(2)

Both functors preserve final objects and commute with fiber products. Therefore they also commute with finite projective limits. With the identification
\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}\cong\mathfrak{Y}_{\text{red},\et}
(idem. for
\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
) from (1), we get a morphism of sites
\mathfrak{X}_{\et}\to\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}
by
[
Sta18
, Tag 04I0]
. There is a natural projection
\nu\colon Y_{\proet}\to Y_{\et}
, so it suffices to show that the natural functor
\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}\to Y_{\et}
induces a morphism of sites
Y_{\et}\to\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}
. This is
[
Hub96
, Lemma 3.5.1(ii)]
.

∎

Lemma
2.1
guarantees that we can use the formalism in
[
ILO14
, Exposé XI]
to describe the underlying site for our product (cf. §1.1 in loc.cit.). This will not be the case for the
q
-crystalline (oriented) product which we deal with in Section
6
. Let
D
be the following site:

(1)

As a category, the objects of
D
are
commutative diagrams

\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{Y}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}}

where
\mathfrak{U}\to\mathfrak{Y}
and
\mathfrak{V}\to\mathfrak{X}
are étale,
W\to Y
is pro-étale, and
W\to\mathfrak{U}
(resp.
Y\to\mathfrak{Y}
) denotes the morphism
W\to U
(resp.
\id\colon Y\to Y
) of objects in
Y_{\proet}
where
U
is the adic generic fiber of
\mathfrak{U}
. We explain the

condition. We demand the right-most square to be a commutative diagram of formal schemes. For the left-most square we demand that the diagram in
Y_{\proet}
:

\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y}

is commutative. For brevity we denote such an object by
(W\to\mathfrak{U}\leftarrow\mathfrak{V})
.

Given two such objects
(W_{1}\to\mathfrak{U}_{1}\leftarrow\mathfrak{V}_{1})
and
(W_{2}\to\mathfrak{U}_{2}\leftarrow\mathfrak{V}_{2})
, a morphism from
(W_{1}\to\mathfrak{U}_{1}\leftarrow\mathfrak{V}_{1})
to

is the datum of three morphisms
W_{1}\to W_{2}
,

and
\mathfrak{V}_{1}\to\mathfrak{V}_{2}
such the diagrams

\textstyle{W_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U_{2},}

\textstyle{\mathfrak{U}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{V}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{V}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

are commutative (here
U_{1}\to U_{2}
is the induced morphism on the adic generic fibers of
\mathfrak{U}_{1}\to\mathfrak{U}_{2}
). For brevity, we will denote such a morphism by the following diagram:

\textstyle{W_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{U}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{V}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{U}_{2}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathfrak{V}_{2}.}

(2)

We equip
D
with the topology generated by coverings

\left\{(W_{i}\to\mathfrak{U}_{i}\leftarrow\mathfrak{V}_{i})\to(W\to\mathfrak{U}\leftarrow\mathfrak{V})\right\}_{i\in I}

of the following types:

(a)

\mathfrak{U}_{i}=\mathfrak{U},\mathfrak{V}_{i}=\mathfrak{V}\text{ }
for all
i\in I
and
(W_{i}\to W)_{i\in I}
is a covering in
Y_{\proet}
,

(b)

W_{i}=W,\mathfrak{U}_{i}=\mathfrak{U}\text{ }
for all
i\in I
and
(\mathfrak{V}_{i}\to\mathfrak{V})_{i\in I}
is a covering in
\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
,

(c)

I
is a singleton and a commutative diagram

\textstyle{W^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{U}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{U}^{\prime}\times_{\mathfrak{U}}\mathfrak{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{U}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathfrak{V},}

(d)

I
is a singleton and a commutative diagram

\textstyle{W^{\prime}=W\times_{U}U^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{U}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{U}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathfrak{V}.}

Notation
.

We will refer to coverings of the above by “type (a)”, “type (b)”, “type (c)” and “type (d)”, respectively.

Let
\widetilde{D}
be the topos of sheaves of sets in
D
. The following is an instance of
[
ILO14
, Exposé XI, Définition 3.3]
.

Definition 2.2
.

The topos
\widetilde{D}
is called the fiber product of
\widetilde{Y_{\proet}}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}_{\et}}
above
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}
, which we denote (via an abuse of language
5
5

5

So as to avoid confusion, we will continue to denote the associated site by
D
. When the context is clear we will drop the tilde from
\widetilde{Y_{\proet}}
,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}_{\et}}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}
to ease notation.
) by

Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
.

One can verify the sheaf condition on
D
as follows.

Lemma 2.3

(
[
ILO14
, Exposé XI, §3.1]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{F}
be a presheaf on
D
. For
\mathscr{F}
to be a sheaf, it suffices to verify the usual conditions of exactness for coverings of type (a) and (b), and for all covering families
Z^{\prime}\to Z
of type (c) and (d),
\mathscr{F}(Z)\to\mathscr{F}(Z^{\prime})
is an isomorphism.

By construction there are natural projections of topoi

p\colon Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}\rightarrow Y_{\proet}\text{ and }q\colon Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}\rightarrow\mathfrak{X}_{\et},

which are given by

p^{-1}(W)=(W\to\mathfrak{Y}\leftarrow\mathfrak{X})\text{ and }q^{-1}(\mathfrak{V})=(Y\to\mathfrak{Y}\leftarrow\mathfrak{V}),

for
W
an object in
Y_{\proet}
and
\mathfrak{V}
an object in
\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
. Moreover the diagram of topoi

\textstyle{Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{Y_{\proet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{Y}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{X}_{\et}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}

admits a canonical 2-isomorphism

\varepsilon\colon fq\to\nu_{Y}p

(2.1)

given by an isomorphism of functors
(fq)_{*}\to(\nu_{Y}p)_{*}
defined as follows: for
\mathscr{F}
a sheaf on
D
and
\mathfrak{V}
an object in
\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}
(with generic fiber
V
),

\varepsilon\colon((fq)_{*}\mathscr{F})(\mathfrak{V})\to((\nu_{Y}p)_{*}\mathscr{F})(\mathfrak{V})

is the composition

\mathscr{F}(Y\to\mathfrak{Y}\leftarrow\mathfrak{V}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}}\mathfrak{X})\to\mathscr{F}(V\to\mathfrak{V}\leftarrow\mathfrak{V}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}}\mathfrak{X})\to\mathscr{F}(V\to\mathfrak{Y}\leftarrow\mathfrak{X})

where the first isomorphism is induced by a covering of type (d) and the second isomorphism is induced by a covering of type (c). In the above diagram, we hope the reader does not find confusing in continuing to use
f
and
\nu_{Y}
to denote the induced morphisms of topoi. We can now state the universal property that
Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
satisfies:

Theorem 2.4

(
[
ILO14
, Exposé XI, Théorème 3.2]
)
.

Let
T
be any topos equipped with morphisms
a\colon T\to Y_{\proet}
and
b\colon T\to\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
and an isomorphism
t\colon fb\xrightarrow{\sim}\nu_{Y}a
. Then there exists a triple
(h\colon T\to Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et},\alpha\colon ph\xrightarrow{\sim}a,\beta\colon qh\xrightarrow{\sim}b)
, unique up to unique isomorphism, such the composition

fb\xrightarrow{\beta^{-1}}fqh\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\nu_{Y}ph\xrightarrow{\alpha}\nu_{Y}a

is equal to
t
. Moreover for
Z=(W\to\mathfrak{U}\leftarrow\mathfrak{V})
an object in
D
,

h^{-1}Z=a^{-1}W\times_{b^{-1}(\mathfrak{U}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}}\mathfrak{X})}b^{-1}\mathfrak{V},

where
a^{-1}W\to b^{-1}(\mathfrak{U}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}}\mathfrak{X})
is the composition
a^{-1}W\to a^{-1}U\xrightarrow{t}b^{-1}(\mathfrak{U}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}}\mathfrak{X})
, where
U
is the generic fiber of
\mathfrak{U}
.

Applying Theoem
2.4
to the topos
X_{\proet}
equipped with the natural morphisms
f_{\eta}\colon X_{\proet}\to Y_{\proet}
and
\nu_{X}\colon X_{\proet}\to\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
gives a morphism of topoi

(\nu_{f}^{-1},\nu_{f*})\colon X_{\proet}\rightarrow Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et},

(2.2)

where

\nu_{f}^{-1}(W\to\mathfrak{U}\leftarrow\mathfrak{V})=(W\times_{Y}X)\times_{(U\times_{Y}X)}\times V=W\times_{U}V.

(2.3)

Definition 2.5
.

We define the completed integral structure sheaf on
D
by the
p
-adically completed tensor product

q^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{X},\et}\widehat{\otimes}_{p^{-1}\nu_{Y}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y},\et}}p^{-1}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+}_{Y}

and denote it by
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+}_{D}
.

Remark 2.6
.

A word of explanation for the definition of
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{D}^{+}
. The morphism
p^{-1}\nu_{Y}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y},\et}\to p^{-1}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+}_{Y}
comes via the natural map of sheaves of rings
\nu_{Y}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y},\et}\to\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+}_{Y}
. By (
2.1
), there is a canonical isomorphism
p^{-1}\nu_{Y}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y},\et}\xrightarrow{\sim}q^{-1}f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y},\et}
. One then composes the latter isomorphism with the morphism
q^{-1}f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y},\et}\to q^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{X},\et}
(coming from the natural map of sheaves of rings
f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y},\et}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{X},\et}
). This procedure produces the morphism
p^{-1}\nu_{Y}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y},\et}\to q^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{X},\et}
.

By construction the projections
p
and
q
extend to morphisms of ringed topoi:

(p,p^{\sharp})\colon(Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et},\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+}_{D})\rightarrow(Y_{\proet},\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+}_{Y})\text{ and }(q,q^{\sharp})\colon(Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et},\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+}_{D})\rightarrow(\mathfrak{X}_{\et},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{X},\et}).

(2.4)

We delay extending
\nu_{f}
to a morphism of ringed topoi to §
5.2
(cf. Proposition
5.7
).

Proposition 2.7
.

(1)

The family of objects of the form
Z:=(\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\to\Spf(S)\leftarrow\Spf(R))
is generating for
D
. Moreover the family is stable under fiber products.

(2)

All objects as in (1) are quasi-compact.

(3)

All objects as in (1) are coherent and the topos
Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
is algebraic (cf.

[
SGA4
, Exposé VI, Définition 2.3]
).

(4)

Assume that
f\colon\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Y}
is qcqs. Then the projection morphism
p\colon Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}\rightarrow Y_{\proet}
is a coherent morphism (cf.
[
SGA4
, Exposé VI, Définition 3.1]
) of algebraic topoi.

Proof.

(1)

Choose any object
T=(W\to\mathfrak{U}\leftarrow\mathfrak{V})
in
D
and fix an affine covering
\{\mathfrak{U}_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{U}
in
\mathfrak{U}_{\et}
. Then
\{\mathfrak{U}_{i}\times_{\mathfrak{U}}\mathfrak{V}\}
is a covering of
\mathfrak{V}
in
\mathfrak{V}_{\et}
. Therefore

\{(W\to\mathfrak{U}\leftarrow\mathfrak{U}_{i}\times_{\mathfrak{U}}\mathfrak{V})\}

is a covering of
T
of type (b). Then by using coverings of type (d):

\textstyle{W^{\prime}=W\times_{U}U_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{U}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{U}_{i}\times_{\mathfrak{U}}\mathfrak{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{U}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathfrak{U}_{i}\times_{\mathfrak{U}}\mathfrak{V},}

where
U
and
U_{i}
are the adic generic fibers of
\mathfrak{U}
and
\mathfrak{U}_{i}
, respectively, we can assume
\mathfrak{U}
is affine. Using coverings of type (b) again, we can assume
\mathfrak{V}
is affine. Finally by using coverings of type (a) and
[
Sch13
, Lemma 3.12(ii)]
, proves that the family is generating. For the last part, note that taking the adic generic fiber commutes with fiber products of formal schemes. Therefore fiber products in
D
are calculated component-wise and now it is clear that the family is stable under fiber products.

(2)

By part (1), the objects appearing there are generating, so we can consider the site whose objects are the objects appearing in (1) together with the coverings of type (a)-(d). This is a site of definition for
Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
. By
[
Sch13
, Lemma 3.12(i)]
,
\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})
is a quasi-compact object in
Y_{\proet}
, and
\Spf(R)
is quasi-compact in
\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
. It follows that all covering families of type (a) or (b) of
Z
have a finite refinement.

(3)

By parts (1) and (2), and the criterion of
[
SGA4
, Exposé VI, Proposition 2.1]
, all objects as in (1) are coherent. We check that (i ter) of Proposition 2.2 in loc.cit. is satisfied. For this we further restrict ourselves to objects of the form

Z^{\prime}:=(\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\to\Spf(S)\leftarrow\Spf(R))

such that
\Spf(S)\to\mathfrak{Y}
(resp.
\Spf(R)\to\mathfrak{X}
) factors over an affine open subset
\mathfrak{Y}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{Y}
(resp.
\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{X}
). This is still a generating family for
D
and

Z^{\prime}\times_{(Y\to\mathfrak{Y}\leftarrow\mathfrak{X})}Z^{\prime}=Z^{\prime}\times_{(Y^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{Y}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathfrak{X}^{\prime})}Z^{\prime},

where
Y^{\prime}
is the (affinoid) adic generic fiber of
\mathfrak{Y}^{\prime}
.

(4)

By part (3) the topos
Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
is algebraic. Moreover by
[
Sch13
, Proposition 3.12(iii)]
,
Y_{\proet}
is an algebraic topos. We use the criterion in
[
SGA4
, Exposé VI, Proposition 3.2]
to check that
p
is a coherent morphism. By
[
Sch13
, Proposition 3.12(ii)-(iii)]
, objects of the form
\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})
are coherent and are a generating family for
Y_{\proet}
. We can further restrict to the class
\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})
that have the additional property that
\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\to Y
factors over an open affinoid
Y^{\prime}\subset Y
where
Y^{\prime}
is the adic generic fiber of some open affine
\mathfrak{Y}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{Y}
. This is still a generating family for
Y_{\proet}
. It suffices to check that

p^{-1}(\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+}))=(\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\to\mathfrak{Y}\leftarrow\mathfrak{X})

(2.5)

is a coherent object of
Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et}
. For this we will use the criterion in
[
SGA4
, Exposé VI, Corollaire 1.17]
. We have a covering of type (c):

\textstyle{\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{Y}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{Y}^{\prime}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}}\mathfrak{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{Y}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}.}

Since the morphism
\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Y}
is quasi-compact and quasi-separated, it follows that
\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{X}
is quasi-compact and quasi-separated. Thus by using coverings of type (b), we obtain a finite covering of (
2.5
)

\{Z_{j}:=(\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\to\mathfrak{Y}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathfrak{X}_{j})\to(\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\to\mathfrak{Y}\leftarrow\mathfrak{X})\}_{j\in J}

where each
\mathfrak{X}_{j}\subset\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}
is affine open (in particular by part (3) each
Z_{j}
is coherent). Moreover for
j,k\in J

Z_{j}\times_{(\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\to\mathfrak{Y}\leftarrow\mathfrak{X})}Z_{k}=(\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\to\mathfrak{Y}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathfrak{X}_{j}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{k}).

(2.6)

Since
\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}
is quasi-separated,
\mathfrak{X}_{j}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{k}
is a finite union of affine opens. By using coverings of type (b) and
[
SGA4
, Exposé VI, Proposition 1.3]
, it follows that the object in (
2.6
) is quasi-compact.

∎

Next we describe a basis for the topology on
D
.

Proposition 2.8
.

The set of object of the form
(\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\to\Spf(S)\leftarrow\Spf(R))
where
W=\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\in Y_{\proet}

is some affinoid perfectoid form a basis for
D
.

Proof.

This is proved in the same way as the first part of Proposition
2.7
(1), except one uses that affinoid perfectoids form a basis for the pro-étale topology (cf.
[
Sch13
, Proposition 4.8]
).
∎

When
f\colon\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Y}
is smooth, we will need a refinement of Proposition
2.8
, in order to relate our upcoming definition of
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
and the relative de Rham complex, via the local computations of Section
4
. For this reason we make the following definition and we assume
f
is smooth for the rest of this section.

Definition 2.9
.

We call an object
Z=(W\to\mathfrak{U}\leftarrow\mathfrak{V})\in D

good
if it is of the form

(\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\to\Spf(S)\leftarrow\Spf(R))

where

(1)

The object
W=\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\in Y_{\proet}

is some affinoid perfectoid.

(2)

For some
d\geq 0
, there is a
\Spf(S)
-étale morphism

\mathfrak{V}=\Spf(R)\rightarrow\Spf(R^{\square})=:\mathfrak{V}^{\square},

where
R^{\square}:=S\{t_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,t_{d}^{\pm 1}\}
.

If in addition
(S_{\infty}[\tfrac{1}{p}])^{\circ}=S_{\infty}
, then we call
Z

very good
. Here
S_{\infty}
is the
p
-adic completion of
\varinjlim_{i\in I}S_{i}^{+}
.

Remark 2.10
.

The last condition “
(S_{\infty}[\tfrac{1}{p}])^{\circ}=S_{\infty}
” of Definition
2.9
may appear somewhat odd at this stage, but is precisely what is required in order to match up (on the nose) the local computations of Section
4
regarding continuous group cohomology and the pushforward of
\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
. Moreover it will turn out that under some finiteness assumptions on
Y
, this condition is always satisfied (cf. Lemma
2.13
).

We will prove that under some mild assumptions,
D
has a basis consisting of very good objects. First we need some preparation.

Lemma 2.11
.

Suppose
T
is a perfectoid
C
-algebra. Then for each
x\in\mathcal{O}
, the
\mathcal{O}
-module
T^{\circ}/x
has no nonzero
\mathfrak{m}
-torsion.

Proof.

This is essentially proven in
[
Sch12
, §5]
. Lemma 5.3(ii) in loc.cit. gives
T^{\circ}\subset(T^{\circ a})_{*}\subset T
, which implies

(T^{\circ a})_{*}[\tfrac{1}{p}]=T.

(2.7)

By Proposition 5.5 in loc.cit.,
T^{\circ a}
is a perfectoid
\mathcal{O}^{a}
-algebra and so by Lemma 5.6 in loc.cit.

(T^{\circ a})_{*}[\tfrac{1}{p}]^{\circ}=(T^{\circ a})_{*}.

(2.8)

Therefore (
2.7
)-(
2.8
) give
T^{\circ}=(T^{\circ a})_{*}
. Therefore by Lemma 5.3(iii) in loc.cit.

T^{\circ}/x=(T^{\circ a})_{*}/x\subset(T^{\circ a}/x)_{*}.

Finally by Proposition 4.4 in loc.cit.
(T^{\circ a}/x)_{*}=\Hom_{\mathcal{O}^{a}}(\mathcal{O}^{a},T^{\circ a}/x)
has no non-zero
\mathfrak{m}
-torsion.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

In the situation of Lemma
2.11
, note that if
(T,T^{+})
is an affinoid pair over
(C,\mathcal{O})
with
T^{+}\subsetneq T^{\circ}
and
0\not=x\in\mathfrak{m}
then
T^{+}/x
does have nonzero
\mathfrak{m}
-torsion. Indeed take
y\in T^{\circ}\backslash T^{+}
. Then
xy\in T^{+}
and its class in the quotient
T^{+}/x
is nonzero
\mathfrak{m}
-torsion.

We are now ready to control powerbounded elements in an affinoid perfectoid. For a related result, cf.
[
MT20
, Example 1.6(iii)]
.

Lemma 2.13
.

Suppose that
Y
is locally of finite type over
\Spa(C,\mathcal{O})
. Then an affinoid perfectoid
\varprojlim_{i\in I}\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})\in Y_{\proet}
satisfies
(S_{\infty}[\tfrac{1}{p}])^{\circ}=S_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Since
Y
is locally of finite type over
\Spa(C,\mathcal{O})
, it follows that
\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{+})
is topologically of finite type over
\Spa(C,\mathcal{O})
and so by
[
Hub94
, Lemma 4.4]

S_{i}^{+}=S_{i}^{\circ}
. The idea of the proof is to modify the terms in the colimit
\varinjlim_{i\in I}S_{i}^{\circ}
, so that the projection morphisms
S_{i}^{\circ}\to S_{\infty}
become well behaved. A clue is that
S_{\infty}
is reduced (as it is a perfectoid ring).

For each
i\in I
, let
S_{i,\text{red}}
be the quotient of
S_{i}
by its nilradical. The quotient morphism
S_{i}\to S_{i,\text{red}}
induces a morphism of adic spaces
\Spa(S_{i,\text{red}},S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ})\to\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{\circ})
which is an isomorphism on the underlying topological spaces (note that
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
is the integral closure of
S_{i}^{\circ}
in
S_{i,\text{red}}
). Furthermore for
j\geq i
we claim that the diagram

\textstyle{\Spa(S_{j,\text{red}},S_{j,\text{red}}^{\circ})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spa(S_{j},S_{j}^{\circ})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spa(S_{i,\text{red}},S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{\circ})}

is cartesian: indeed the ring morphism
S_{i}\to S_{j}
is finite étale and so
S_{j,\text{red}}=S_{i,\text{red}}\otimes_{S_{i}}S_{j}
. Moreover, again by Lemma 4.4 in loc.cit.,
S_{j,\text{red}}^{\circ}
is the integral closure of
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}\otimes_{S_{i}^{\circ}}S_{j}^{\circ}
in
S_{j,\text{red}}
. In particular the morphism
\Spa(S_{j,\text{red}},S_{j,\text{red}}^{\circ})\to\Spa(S_{i,\text{red}},S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ})
is surjective. This implies that for each
n\geq 1
,
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}/p^{n}\to S_{j,\text{red}}^{\circ}/p^{n}
is injective and therefore after taking limits, the same is true for
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}\to S_{j,\text{red}}^{\circ}
(for each
i\in I
,
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
is reduced and so by
[
BGR84
, §6.2.4, Theorem 1]
,
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
is bounded and in particular a ring of definition for
S_{i,\text{red}}
equipped with the
p
-adic topology).

The morphisms
S_{i}\to S_{\infty}[\tfrac{1}{p}]
factor through
S_{i,\text{red}}
(as
S_{\infty}[\tfrac{1}{p}]
is a perfectoid
C
-algebra and in particular reduced). Therefore
S_{i}^{\circ}\to S_{\infty}
factors through
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
. We will need to compare
\varinjlim_{i\in I}S_{i}^{\circ}
and
\varinjlim_{i\in I}S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
. For this we show:

Lemma 2.14
.

The ring morphism
S_{i}^{\circ}\to S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
is surjective.

Proof.

Recall that the morphism
\Spa(S_{i,\text{red}},S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ})\to\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{\circ})
is an isomorphism on topological spaces.
The morphism
S_{i}\to S_{i,\text{red}}
is surjective. For
s\in S_{i}\backslash S_{i}^{\circ}
, there exists
x\in\Spa(S_{i},S_{i}^{\circ})
such that
\lvert x(s)\lvert>1
. The preimage
x^{\prime}\in\Spa(S_{i,\text{red}},S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ})
of
x
has the property that
\lvert x^{\prime}(s^{\prime})\lvert=\lvert x(s)\lvert>1
, where
s^{\prime}\in S_{i,\text{red}}
the image of
s
. Therefore
s^{\prime}\in S_{i,\text{red}}\backslash S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
and the induced morphism
S_{i}^{\circ}\to S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
is surjective.
∎

By Lemma
2.14
, it follows that
S_{\infty}
coincides with the
p
-adic completion of
\varinjlim_{i\in I}S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
: indeed we obtain a surjection
\varinjlim_{i\in I}S_{i}^{\circ}\to\varinjlim_{i\in I}S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
which induces a surjection on the
p
-adic completions (cf.
[
Sta18
, Tag 0315]
). Finally the composition
(\varinjlim_{i\in I}S_{i}^{\circ})^{\wedge}\to(\varinjlim_{i\in I}S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ})^{\wedge}\to S_{\infty}
is an isomorphism.

Since
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
is a subring of
S_{i,\text{red}}
(a
C
-algebra), it follows that
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
is torsionfree as an
\mathcal{O}
-module and hence flat. Moreover by the finiteness theorem of Grauert-Remmert (cf.
[
BGR84
, §6.4.1, Corollary 5]
), it follows that
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
is topologically of finite type over
\mathcal{O}
(cf.
[
Sch13
, Theorem 9.4]
). By a flattening theorem of Raynaud-Gruson, flatness together with topologically of finite type implies topologically of finite presentation (cf.
[
Bos14
, §7.3, Corollary 5]
) and so
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
is topologically of finite presentation over
\mathcal{O}
.

Therefore by
[
BMS18
, Lemma 8.10]
6
6

6

Technically the result in loc.cit. assumes that
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
is
p
-completely smooth
\mathcal{O}
-algebra, however the same argument works under the slightly weaker assumptions:

(1)

S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}/p
is finitely presented over
\mathcal{O}/p
and

(2)

S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
is flat over
\mathcal{O}
.

,
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}
is the
p
-adic completion of a free
\mathcal{O}
-module. In particular for each
0\not=x\in\mathfrak{m}
, the
\mathcal{O}
-modules
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}/x
have no nonzero
\mathfrak{m}
-torsion. The surjectivity of
\Spa(S_{j,\text{red}},S_{j,\text{red}}^{\circ})\to\Spa(S_{i,\text{red}},S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ})
implies
S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}/x\to S_{j,\text{red}}^{\circ}/x
is injective. It follows that the direct limit
\varinjlim_{i\in I}S_{i,\text{red}}^{\circ}/x
has no nonzero
\mathfrak{m}
-torsion. Therefore
S_{\infty}/x
has no nonzero
\mathfrak{m}
-torsion. The result now follows from Remark
2.12
.
∎

Lemma 2.15
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{V}\to\Spf(S)
is a smooth morphism of
p
-adic formal schemes over
\mathcal{O}
. Then locally for the Zariski topology on
\mathfrak{V}
, the morphism factors as

\Spf(R)\to\Spf(S\{t_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,t_{d}^{\pm 1}\})\to\Spf(S),

where the first morphism is étale (for some
d\geq 0
).

Proof.

This is the essentially the same argument as
[
Bha18
, Lemma 4.9]
(in the case
S=\mathcal{O}
the argument in loc.cit. shows that it can be arranged so that the first morphism
\Spf(R)\to\Spf(S\{t_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,t_{d}^{\pm 1}\})
is finite étale).
∎

Proposition 2.16
.

(1)

The set of
Z\in D
which are good form a basis for
D
.

(2)

If in addition to (1),
Y
is locally of finite type over
\Spa(C,\mathcal{O})
, then a good object is very good.

Proof.

The first statement follows from Proposition
2.8
together with Lemma
2.15
(by using coverings of type (b)). Finally the last statement follows from Lemma
2.13
.
∎

2.2
The object
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}

In this section, let
\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathfrak{Y}
be any flat
p
-adic formal schemes of type (S)(b) over
\mathcal{O}
, as in
[
Hub96
, §1.9]
. Let
f\colon\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Y}
be any
\Spf(\mathcal{O})
-morphism. Considering
\nu_{f}
and
p
as the following morphism of ringed topoi

(\nu_{f},\nu_{f}^{\sharp})\colon(X_{\proet},A_{\Inf})\rightarrow(Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et},A_{\Inf})\text{ and }(p,p^{\sharp})\colon(Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et},A_{\Inf})\rightarrow(Y_{\proet},A_{\Inf}),

respectively, we make the following definition.

Definition 2.17
.

The relative complex
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}\in D(Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et},A_{\Inf})
is defined by

A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}:=L\eta_{\mu}(R\nu_{f*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}).

In addition we set
R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}):=Rp_{*}A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
, an object living in
D(Y_{\proet},A_{\Inf})
. We call
R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y})
the (relative)
A_{\Inf}
-cohomology.

We will sometimes need to
upgrade
Definition
2.17
and consider
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
as an object over the sheaf of Witt vectors. To make this precise we now consider
\nu_{f}
and
p
as the following morphism of ringed topoi

(\nu_{f}^{W},\nu_{f}^{W\sharp})\colon(X_{\proet},W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{X}))\to(Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et},p^{-1}W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{Y}))

and

(p^{W},p^{W\sharp})\colon(Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et},p^{-1}W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{Y}))\rightarrow(Y_{\proet},W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{Y})),

and we make the following definition (note that the ideal sheaf
p^{-1}((\mu))\subset p^{-1}W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{Y})
is invertible).

Definition 2.18
.

By considering
p
-adic derived completion taking place in the ringed site
(X_{\proet},W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{X}))
we can
consider
\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
as an object of
D(X_{\proet},W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{X}))
, in which case we denote it by
\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}^{W}
. The relative complex
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}^{W}\in D(D,p^{-1}W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{Y}))
is defined by

A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}^{W}:=L\eta_{p^{-1}((\mu))}(R\nu^{W}_{f*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}^{W}).

In addition we set
R\Gamma^{W}_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}):=Rp^{W}_{*}A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}^{W}
, an object living in
D(Y_{\proet},W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{Y}))
.

We will now check that
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
and
R\Gamma_{A_{\Inf}}(\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y})
are compatible with their
W
-variants (after which we will drop the superscript
W
from the notation when context is clear).
Let

(r,r^{\sharp})\colon(Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et},p^{-1}W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{Y}))\to(Y_{\proet}\times_{\mathfrak{Y}_{\et}}\mathfrak{X}_{\et},A_{\Inf})

and

s\colon(X_{\proet},W(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{+,\flat}_{X}))\to(X_{\proet},A_{\Inf})

be the natural morphisms of ringed topoi.

Lemma 2.19
.

There is a natural quasi-isomorphism
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}\cong Rr_{*}A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}^{W}
.

Proof.

Note that
Rs_{*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}^{W}=\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
(as
p
-adic derived completion commutes with forgetting
W
) and since
\nu_{f}\circ s=r\circ v_{f}^{W}
we obtain
R\nu_{f*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}=Rr_{*}R\nu_{f*}^{W}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}^{W}
. Therefore
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}=L\eta_{\mu}Rr_{*}R\nu_{f*}^{W}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}^{W}
. Pick a
p^{-1}((\mu))
-torsion-free resolution
C^{\bullet}
of
R\nu_{f*}^{W}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}^{W}
in the sense of
[
BMS18
, pg. 288]
. Since
r_{*}
is an exact functor (because it is identity at the level of topoi), it follows that
r_{*}C^{\bullet}
is a
(\mu)
-torsion free complex representing
Rr_{*}C^{\bullet}
. Thus
L\eta_{\mu}Rr_{*}C^{\bullet}
is represented by the complex
\eta_{\mu}r_{*}C^{\bullet}
. Finally note that
\eta_{\mu}r_{*}=r_{*}\eta_{p^{-1}((\mu))}
.
∎

In the case
f
is smooth and
\mathfrak{Y}=\Spf(\mathcal{O})
, we can compare our objects to those of
[
BMS18
]
. We delay this to Section
5
after the local computations have been made, cf. Proposition
5.15
.

2.3
Almost
\mathcal{O}
-sheaves

In this section, we will recall the notion of
\alpha
-sheaves (or almost sheaves) as introduced in
[
AG22
, §2.6]
. There is also recent work of
[
Zav21
]
. Throughout we will fix some site
\mathscr{C}
and employ the notation in
[
GR03
]
. The following is an instance of
[
AG22
, Définition 2.6.16]
.

Definition 2.20
.

We call the category of
presheaves of

\mathcal{O}^{a}
-
modules
(
or almost
\mathcal{O}
-presheaves
) on
\mathscr{C}
to be the category of presheaves on
\mathscr{C}
whose values are in the category
\mathcal{O}^{a}
. We denote this category by
\widehat{\mathscr{C}}^{a}
. We call an isomorphism in
\widehat{\mathscr{C}}^{a}
an almost isomorphism.

Armed with the notion of an almost
\mathcal{O}
-presheaf, we now define almost
\mathcal{O}
-sheaves, cf. Définition 2.6.24 in loc.cit.

Definition 2.21
.

An object
\mathscr{F}
in
\widehat{\mathscr{C}}^{a}
is called an
almost
\mathcal{O}
-sheaf
if for each object
X
of
\mathscr{C}
and for each covering family
\{U_{i}\}_{i}
of
X
the canonical morphism

\mathscr{F}(X)\to\varprojlim_{V\to U_{i}}\mathscr{F}(V)

is an isomorphism. We denote
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}^{a}
the full subcategory of
\widehat{\mathscr{C}}^{a}
formed by the almost
\mathcal{O}
-sheaves and
\iota\colon\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}^{a}\to\widehat{\mathscr{C}}^{a}
, the natural inclusion.

There is a natural sheafification functor, cf. Proposition 2.6.31 and Proposition 2.6.33 in loc.cit.

Proposition 2.22
.

The inclusion
\iota\colon\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}^{a}\to\widehat{\mathscr{C}}^{a}
admits a left-adjoint

\overline{a}\colon\widehat{\mathscr{C}}^{a}\to\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}^{a}

and is exact.

We will need to compare the categories
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}^{a}
and
\widehat{\mathscr{C}}^{a}
to the usual categories of presheaves and sheaves of
\mathcal{O}
-modules.

Definition 2.23
.

We denote by
\Mod_{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}})
(resp.
\Mod_{\mathcal{O}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{C}})
) the category of preheaves (resp. sheaves) in
\mathcal{O}
-modules on
\mathscr{C}
and
i\colon\Mod_{\mathcal{O}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{C}})\to\Mod_{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}})
the natural inclusion.

The categories
\Mod_{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}})
(resp.
\Mod_{\mathcal{O}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{C}})
) together with their almost counterparts
\widehat{\mathscr{C}}^{a}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}^{a}
) are abelian cf.
[
Sta18
, Tag 01AG]
and
[
AG22
, Corollaire 2.6.18, Proposition 2.6.34]
. Thus one can define their associated derived categories. We will denote the derived category of
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}^{a}
by
D(\mathscr{C},\mathcal{O}^{a})
.

Definition 2.24
.

We call a morphism
\mathscr{F}\to\mathscr{G}
in
D(\mathscr{C},\mathcal{O}^{a})
or
D(\widehat{\mathscr{C}}^{a})
an
almost isomorphism
if it is a quasi-isomorphism. For
\mathscr{F}\in D(\mathscr{C},\mathcal{O})
, let
\mathscr{F}^{a}
be its image in
D(\mathscr{C},\mathcal{O}^{a})
.

The localization functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}-\text{mod}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{O}^{a}-\text{mod}

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\mapsto M^{a}

induces the exact functors

\widehat{\alpha}\colon\Mod_{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}})\to\widehat{\mathscr{C}}^{a}\text{ and }\widetilde{\alpha}\colon\Mod_{\mathcal{O}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{C}})\to\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}^{a}.

The next proposition says, in particular, that to verify whether a morphism of almost
\mathcal{O}
-sheaves is an almost isomorphism, it suffices to verify it on the level of presheaves, cf. Proposition 2.6.36 in loc.cit.

Proposition 2.25
.

The diagram

\textstyle{\Mod_{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\alpha}}
\textstyle{\Mod_{\mathcal{O}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathscr{C}}^{a}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}^{a}}

is commutative, up to unique isomorphism (where
a\colon\Mod_{\mathcal{O}}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}})\to\Mod_{\mathcal{O}}(\widetilde{\mathscr{C}})
is the usual sheafification).

2.4
Miscellany

In this short section we collect some properties of the ring
A_{\Inf}
(and its relative version) for which we could not find a reference for.

Lemma 2.26
.

The tensor product
W(\mathfrak{m}^{\flat})\otimes_{A_{\Inf},\theta\circ\varphi^{-1}}\mathcal{O}
identifies with
\mathfrak{m}
.

Proof.

From the exact sequence

0\to W(\mathfrak{m}^{\flat})\to A_{\Inf}\to W(k)\to 0

it suffices to show
\textnormal{Tor}_{A_{\Inf}}^{1}(W(k),A_{\Inf}/\tilde{\xi})
vanishes. The image of
\tilde{\xi}
in
W(k)
is
p
. Since
W(k)
is
p
-torsion free, the result follows.
∎

In addition to the site
X_{\proet}
, we consider the site
X_{\proet}^{\text{psh}}
, whose objects are affinoid perfectoids in
X_{\proet}
, and coverings are isomorphisms. In this way, every presheaf is already a sheaf. We denote the associated topos by
X_{\proet}^{\text{psh}}
. There is a morphism of topoi

(\phi^{-1},\phi_{*})\colon X_{\proet}\to X_{\proet}^{\text{psh}},

for which
\phi_{*}
is given by restricting sheaves on
X_{\proet}
to
X_{\proet}^{\text{psh}}
and
\phi^{-1}
sheafification.

Lemma 2.27
.

The
R\phi_{*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
is derived
(p,\mu)
-adically complete in the presheaf topos.

Proof.

By
[
Sta18
, Tag 0BLX]
, it suffices to show that for each affinoid perfectoid
U\in X_{\proet}
,
R\Gamma(U,\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X})
is derived
(p,\mu)
-adically complete. It is certainly derived
p
-adically complete by definition, so it suffices to show it is derived
\mu
-adically complete. Since we are in the presheaf topos (it is replete), by
[
BMS18
, Lemma 6.15]
, it suffices to check each
H^{i}(U,\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X})
is derived
\mu
-adically complete. Now
\mu\in W(\mathfrak{m}^{\flat})
and
H^{i}(U,\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X})
is killed by
W(\mathfrak{m}^{\flat})
for
i>0
(cf. the statement just after
[
BMS18
, Lemma 5.6]
). Therefore
H^{i}(U,\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X})
is derived
\mu
-adically complete for
i>0
. For
i=0
, one has
H^{0}(U,\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X})=W(R^{\flat+})
where
\Spa(R,R^{+})
is the perfectoid space determined by
U
and this is even classically
\mu
-adically complete.
∎

Corollary 2.28
.

The object
\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
is derived
\tilde{\xi}
-adically complete.

Proof.

Since
\phi^{-1}\circ R\phi_{*}\cong\id
, this is an immediate consequence of Lemma
2.27
(note that
\tilde{\xi}\in(p,\mu)
).
∎

3
Relative
p
-adic étale comparison

In this section we record the relative
p
-adic étale comparison (cf.
[
BMS18
, Theorem 14.3(iv)]
and
[
CK19
, Theorem 2.3]
) in Theorem
3.15
. The proof follows the same strategy as those in loc.cit., although in the current (relative) situation, the relevant statements are formulated in terms of complexes of
sheaves
rather than modules. For this reason we need to check certain complexes of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-sheaves are well behaved and we begin with some preliminary work guaranteeing this.

3.1
Lisse
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-sheaves

In this section, unless otherwise stated,
X
will temporarily denote a locally noetherian adic space over
\Spa(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. In
[
Sch13
, Definition 8.1]
, Scholze defines the notion of a lisse
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-sheaf on
X_{\proet}
. Roughly speaking a lisse
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-sheaf on
X_{\proet}
is a literal projective limit of a classical lisse
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-sheaf appearing on the étale site
X_{\et}
(cf. Proposition 8.2 in loc.cit.). This is possible because (derived) limits on the pro-étale site are well behaved, in that they (usually) become limits. Much like in
[
BS15
]
where the authors define and study the notion of a
constructible complex
on the pro-étale site of a qcqs scheme, we will do the same for lisse
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-sheaves. We follow closely the framework developed in
[
Sta18
, Tag 09C0]
.

Notation
.

For
K\in D(X_{\proet},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
we set
K_{n}:=K\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
. We write
\underline{M}
for the image of
M\in D(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
under the pullback
D(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to D(X_{\proet},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

Definition 3.1
.

An object
K\in D(X_{\proet},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is called
lisse
if:

(1)

K
is
p
-adically derived complete and

(2)

K_{1}
has finite locally constant cohomology sheaves and locally has finite tor dimension.

Remark 3.2
.

By
[
Sch13
, Proposition 8.2]
, local systems of
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-modules on
X_{\proet}
are equivalent (via pullback) to local systems of
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-modules on
X_{\et}
. By Corollary 3.17(i) in loc.cit. the pushforward is a quasi-inverse. A detailed proof can be found in
[
Zav21
, Theorem C.4]
7
7

7

In the beginning of
[
Zav21
, Appendix C]
, Zavyalov makes the assumption that
X
is a locally
strongly
noetherian adic space over
\Spa(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, but the “
strongly
” assumption is not essential.
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose
K
is lisse and
X
is quasi-compact. There exists
a
and
b
such that

(1)

each
K_{n}
has tor-amplitude in
[a,b]
,

(2)

each
K_{n}
has finite locally constant cohomology sheaves,

(3)

H^{i}(K)=\varprojlim H^{i}(K_{n})
,

(4)

H^{i}(K)=0
for
i\notin[a,b]
,

(5)

each
K_{n}
is obtained via pullback of a bounded
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-complex with finite locally constant cohomology sheaves from
X_{\et}
.

Proof.

(1)

Since
X
is quasi-compact and pro-étale morphisms are open, it follows that we can find
a\leq b
such that
K_{1}
has tor amplitude in
[a,b]
. For the remaining
K_{n}
, the result follows from
[
Sta18
, Tag 0942]
.

(2)

The proof is the same as
[
Sta18
, Tag 094I]
.

(3)

Since
K
is
p
-adically derived complete
K=R\varprojlim K_{n}
. A priori, by part (2) each
K_{n}
has finite locally constant cohomology sheaves on
X_{\proet}
, but by Remark
3.2
,
they can be considered as finite locally constant sheaves on
X_{\et}
. The result now follows from
[
Sch13
, Theorem 4.9]
and
[
Sta18
, Tag 0A09]
.

(4)

This follows from parts (1) and (3).

(5)

This is an application of
[
Sta18
, Tag 0D7U]
to Remark
3.2
.

∎

It will useful to strengthen part (5) of Lemma
3.3
to
X_{\fet}
:

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
X=\Spa(A,A^{+})
be an affinoid
connected noetherian adic space over
\Spa(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. Suppose
K\in D(X_{\proet},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is lisse. Then each
K_{n}
is obtained via pullback of a bounded
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-complex with finite locally constant cohomology sheaves from
X_{\fet}
.

Proof.

Indeed by part (5) of Lemma
3.3
, it suffices to verify the conditions of
[
Sta18
, Tag 0D7U]
(comparing finite locally constant sheaves on
X_{\et}
and
X_{\text{f}\et}
). This is achieved in
[
Sch13
, Theorem 4.9]
.
∎

Definition 3.5
.

An object
K\in D(X_{\proet},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is called
adic lisse
if
K
is locally isomorphic to

\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}^{\mathbb{L}}\underline{M}

(3.1)

where
M\in D(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
a finite complex of finite free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-modules.

For our next task, we want to compare the notions of lisse and adic lisse. It is not difficult to trivialize each
K_{n}
one by one. The key idea however is to control these trivializations as one jumps from
n
to
n+1
. For instance, in the setting of
[
BS15
]
, this is achieved via the notion of
w-strictly local affine scheme
(cf. Lemma 6.6.8 in loc.cit.). In the current setup, we don’t quite have access to such a nice basis (it’s unlikely that
X_{\proet}
gives rise to a replete topos), but nevertheless an analogue does exist via
[
Sch13
, Theorem 4.9]
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
X=\Spa(A,A^{+})
be an affinoid
connected noetherian adic space over
\Spa(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. Suppose
K\in D(X_{\proet},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is lisse. Then there exists a pro-(finite étale) cover
U\to X
trivializing all
K_{n}
(i.e. there are perfect complexes
M_{n}\in D(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
such that
K_{n}\lvert_{U}\cong\underline{M_{n}}\lvert_{U}
).

Proof.

We will use the results of Lemma
3.3
without mention. The idea is to first trivialize
K_{1}
for a finite étale covering
U_{1}\to X
and then construct a sequence of finite étale covers
U_{n+1}\to U_{n}
trivializing
K_{n+1}
. We proceed by induction on
n
.

Let us deal with
K_{1}
first. We produce a finite étale cover
U_{1}\to X
and a perfect complex of
\mathbb{Z}/p
-modules
M_{1}
such that
K_{1}\lvert_{U_{1}}\cong\underline{M_{1}}\lvert_{U_{1}}
. By Lemma
3.4
, first note that
K_{1}
comes (via pullback) of a complex in
D(X_{\fet},\mathbb{Z}/p)
which has finite locally constant cohomology sheaves (in
X_{\fet}
). By
[
Sta18
, Tag 094G]
we can choose a finite étale cover
U_{1}\to X
(with
U_{1}
affinoid connected) such that
K_{1}\lvert_{U_{1}}\cong\underline{M_{1}}\lvert_{U_{1}}
for some finite complex of finite
\mathbb{Z}/p
-modules
M_{1}
.

Suppose we have trivialized
K_{n}
via surjective finite étale maps
U_{n}\to U_{1}
with each
U_{n}
affinoid connected. Then repeating the argument for
K_{1}
with
X
replaced by
U_{n}
, we produce a surjective finite étale map
U_{(n+1)}\to U_{n}
trivializing
K_{n+1}
with
U_{(n+1)}
affinoid connected.

In summary we have produced a sequence of finite étale connected (affinoid) covers
U_{n+1}\to U_{n}
, trivializing
K_{n+1}
. The pro-object
U:=\varprojlim U_{n}
then gives a pro-(finite étale) cover trivializing all
K_{n}
.
∎

Recall that for
X
an affinoid connected noetherian adic space over
\Spa(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, there is a pro-object
X_{\infty}:=\varprojlim_{i}X_{i}\to X
constructed in
[
Sch13
, Theorem 4.9]
8
8

8

Technically in loc.cit.
X_{\infty}
is used to denote the
associated
perfectoid affinoid space (to the pro-object). In our exposition we will denote the pro-object by
X_{\infty}
.
. This is called the
universal cover
of
X
(the
X_{i}
are the Galois objects in
X_{\fet}
) and it is indeed a cover in
X_{\proet}
as explained in
[
Sch16
]
. The point being that for
G
a profinite group, the map
G\to*
is a cover in
G
-pfsets. One then uses
[
Sch13
, Proposition 3.5]
to give a covering
X_{\infty}\to X
in
X_{\proet}
(even in
X_{\profet}
).

Corollary 3.7
.

In the situation of Lemma
3.6
, the universal cover
X_{\infty}\to X
of
X
trivializes all
K_{n}
. Moreover in this case we have canonical trivializations:
R\Gamma(X_{\infty},K_{n})\in D(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
is a perfect complex and the canonical morphism

\underline{R\Gamma(X_{\infty},K_{n})}\to K_{n}\lvert_{X_{\infty}}

is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

For the first part, note that the universal cover
X_{\infty}
covers
U
(appearing in Lemma
3.6
). Thus by Lemma
3.6
,
K_{n}\lvert_{X_{\infty}}\cong\underline{M_{n}}
for some perfect complex
M_{n}\in D(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
. Fix such a trivialization. Then by naturality it suffices to show that the canonical morphism

\underline{R\Gamma(X_{\infty},\underline{M_{n}})}\to\underline{M_{n}}\lvert_{X_{\infty}}

is a quasi-isomorphism. This follows because taking sections of constant sheaves of
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-modules over
X_{\infty}
is exact (cf. proof of
[
Sch12
, Theorem 4.9]
) and
X_{\infty}
is connected (i.e.
H^{0}(X_{\infty},\mathbb{F}_{p})=\mathbb{F}_{p}
). Finally perfectness of
M_{n}
, implies the perfectness of
R\Gamma(X_{\infty},K_{n})
.
∎

The trivializations constructed in Lemma
3.6
are not neccessarily compatible with the transition morphisms
K_{n+1}\to K_{n}
. On the other hand, Corollary
3.7
provides a fix for this. The reason is that
X_{\infty}
is at the same time connected and a kind of
weakly contractible
object for local systems of
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-modules. This simplicity is in contrast to the situation in
[
BS15
]
, where weakly contractible objects are rarely connected. The authors in loc.cit. must therefore work harder in order to glue the trivializations (for each
K_{n}
) together and this is solved by Lemma 6.6.2 in loc.cit.

We now come to the main result of this section.

Proposition 3.8
.

Suppose
K\in D(X_{\proet},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is lisse. Then
K
is adic lisse.

Proof.

Working locally, we can assume that
X=\Spa(A,A^{+})
is affinoid connected. Then by Corollary
3.7
,

K\lvert_{X_{\infty}}=R\varprojlim K_{n}|_{X_{\infty}}\cong R\varprojlim\underline{R\Gamma(X_{\infty},K_{n})}.

(3.2)

By
[
Sta18
, Tag 09AW]
we can find a finite complex of finite free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-modules
M
and isomorphisms
M\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\cong R\Gamma(X_{\infty},K_{n})
compatible with the transition map
R\Gamma(X_{\infty},K_{n+1})\to R\Gamma(X_{\infty},K_{n})
. Therefore (
3.2
) implies

K\lvert_{X_{\infty}}\cong R\varprojlim(\underline{M}\lvert_{X_{\infty}}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}^{\mathbb{L}}\underline{M}\lvert_{X_{\infty}}.

∎

3.2
The comparison isomorphism

In this section we assume that
f\colon\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Y}
is a proper morphism of
p
-adic formal schemes locally of finite type and flat over
\mathcal{O}
such that the induced morphism on the adic generic fibers
f_{\eta}\colon X\to Y
is smooth. Note that in this case
X
and
Y
are locally of finite type over
\Spa(C,\mathcal{O})
and are rigid spaces (considered as adic spaces). Moreover
f_{\eta}
is also proper.

Remark 3.9
.

In fact if
\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{Y}^{\prime}
are formal schemes locally of finite type and flat over
\mathcal{O}
, and
f^{\prime}\colon\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{Y}^{\prime}
an
\mathcal{O}
-morphism, then by
[
Hub96
, Remark 1.3.18(ii)]
, the properness of
f^{\prime}
is equivalent to the properness of
f^{\prime}_{\eta}
.

With the framework established in §
3.1
, we are ready to prove the relative
p
-adic étale comparison (cf. Theorem
3.15
).

Lemma 3.10
.

We have an identification

A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}\otimes_{A_{\Inf}}^{\mathbb{L}}A_{\Inf}[\tfrac{1}{\mu}]\cong R\nu_{f*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}\otimes_{A_{\Inf}}^{\mathbb{L}}A_{\Inf}[\tfrac{1}{\mu}]

(3.3)

Proof.

The localization morphism
A_{\Inf}\to A_{\Inf}[\tfrac{1}{\mu}]
is flat, so the claim is a consequence of
[
BMS18
, Lemma 6.14]
.
∎

For completeness we also record the following completed version, which will be useful when comparing
q
-crystalline cohomology with
p
-adic étale cohomology (cf. Theorem
6.36
). By
[
Sta18
, Tag 0A0G]

R\nu_{f*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
is
p
-adically derived complete and so the natural morphism
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}\to R\nu_{f*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
induces a morphism

A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}^{\wedge}\to R\nu_{f*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X},

(3.4)

where
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}^{\wedge}
is the
p
-adic derived completion of
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}
.

Lemma 3.11
.

The morphism

A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}^{\wedge}\otimes_{A_{\Inf}}^{\mathbb{L}}A_{\Inf}[\tfrac{1}{\mu}]\to R\nu_{f*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}\otimes_{A_{\Inf}}^{\mathbb{L}}A_{\Inf}[\tfrac{1}{\mu}]

(3.5)

induced by (
3.4
) is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

This requires a slightly more delicate analysis compared to its uncompleted version in Lemma
3.10
. Let
C^{\bullet}
be the cone of canonical morphism
A\Omega_{\mathfrak{X}/\mathfrak{Y}}\to R\nu_{f*}\mathbb{A}_{\Inf,X}
given by
[
